</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Lutheranism" title="Template:Lutheranism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Lutheranism" title="Template talk:Lutheranism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Lutheranism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Dietrich Bonhoeffer</b> (<small>German:&#32;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="de-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Standard_German" title="Help:IPA/Standard German">[ˈdiːtʁɪç ˈbɔnhøːfɐ]</a></span>&#32;<span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="ext-phonos ext-phonos-PhonosButton noexcerpt ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/commons\/transcoded\/f\/f8\/De-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer.oga\/De-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer.oga.mp3&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;De-Dietrich Bonhoeffer.oga&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;ext-phonos&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;noexcerpt&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/f/f8/De-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer.oga/De-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer.oga.mp3" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt"><a href="/wiki/File:De-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer.oga" title="File:De-Dietrich Bonhoeffer.oga">i</a></sup>; 4 February 1906&#160;– 9 April 1945) was a German <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheran</a> pastor, theologian and anti-<a href="/wiki/Nazism" title="Nazism">Nazi</a> dissident who was a key founding member of the <a href="/wiki/Confessing_Church" title="Confessing Church">Confessing Church</a>. His writings on Christianity's role in the secular world have become widely influential; his 1937 book <i><a href="/wiki/The_Cost_of_Discipleship" title="The Cost of Discipleship">The Cost of Discipleship</a></i> is described as a modern classic.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Apart from his theological writings, Bonhoeffer was known for his staunch resistance to the Nazi dictatorship, including vocal opposition to <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Euthanasia" title="Euthanasia">euthanasia</a> program and genocidal persecution of the Jews.<sup id="cite_ref-Rasmussen_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rasmussen-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He was arrested in April 1943 by the <a href="/wiki/Gestapo" title="Gestapo">Gestapo</a> and imprisoned at <a href="/wiki/Tegel_Prison" title="Tegel Prison">Tegel Prison</a> for one-and-a-half years. Later, he was transferred to <a href="/wiki/Flossenb%C3%BCrg_concentration_camp" title="Flossenbürg concentration camp">Flossenbürg concentration camp</a>.
</p><p>Bonhoeffer was accused of being associated with the <a href="/wiki/20_July_plot" title="20 July plot">20 July plot</a> to assassinate Hitler and was tried along with other accused plotters, including former members of the <a href="/wiki/Abwehr" title="Abwehr">Abwehr</a> (the German Military Intelligence Office). He was hanged on 9 April 1945 during the collapse of the Nazi regime.
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r886046785">.mw-parser-output .toclimit-2 .toclevel-1 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-3 .toclevel-2 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-4 .toclevel-3 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-5 .toclevel-4 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-6 .toclevel-5 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-7 .toclevel-6 ul{display:none}</style><div class="toclimit-3"><meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" /></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood_and_family">Childhood and family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Childhood and family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bonhoeffer was born on 4 February 1906 in <a href="/wiki/Wroclaw" class="mw-redirect" title="Wroclaw">Breslau</a>, then Germany (now Poland), into a large family.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> In addition to his other siblings, Dietrich had a twin sister, <a href="/wiki/Bonhoeffer_family" title="Bonhoeffer family">Sabine Bonhoeffer Leibholz</a>: he and Sabine were the sixth and seventh children out of eight.<sup id="cite_ref-:12_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> His father was <a href="/wiki/Karl_Bonhoeffer" title="Karl Bonhoeffer">Karl Bonhoeffer</a>, a psychiatrist and neurologist, noted for his criticism of <a href="/wiki/Sigmund_Freud" title="Sigmund Freud">Sigmund Freud</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-:22_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:22-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> and his mother Paula Bonhoeffer was a teacher and the granddaughter of Protestant theologian <a href="/wiki/Karl_Hase" title="Karl Hase">Karl von Hase</a> and painter <a href="/wiki/Stanislaus_von_Kalckreuth" title="Stanislaus von Kalckreuth">Stanislaus von Kalckreuth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Bonhoeffer's family dynamic and his parents' values enabled him to receive a high level of education and encouraged his curiosity, which impacted his ability to lead others around him, specifically in the church setting.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He also learned how to play the piano at age 8, and composed the songs performed at the Philharmonic at age 11.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Walter Bonhoeffer, the second born of the Bonhoeffer family, was killed in action during <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> when Bonhoeffer was 12 years old.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> At age 14, Bonhoeffer decided to pursue his education in theology despite the criticism of his older brothers Klaus, a lawyer, and Karl, a scientist.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He took Hebrew as an elective in school and attended many evangelical meetings, moved by the toils of war and hungered children.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Bonhoeffer began his studies at Tübingen and eventually moved to the University of Berlin, where he submitted his successful dissertation: Sanctorum Communio.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> At the age of 21, on 17 December 1927, he went on to complete his Doctor of Theology degree from <a href="/wiki/Humboldt_University_of_Berlin" title="Humboldt University of Berlin">Humboldt University of Berlin</a>, graduating <i><a href="/wiki/Summa_cum_laude" class="mw-redirect" title="Summa cum laude">summa cum laude</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Studies_in_America">Studies in America</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Studies in America">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/1930" title="1930">1930</a> Bonhoeffer moved to America with the interest of attaining a Sloane Fellowship at New York City's <a href="/wiki/Union_Theological_Seminary_(New_York_City)" title="Union Theological Seminary (New York City)">Union Theological Seminary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:122_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:122-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Bonhoeffer was greatly unimpressed with American theology. He described the students as lacking interest in theology and would "laugh out loud" when learning a passage from Luther's Sin and Forgiveness.<sup id="cite_ref-:222_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:222-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> During his time there, he met Frank Fisher, a black fellow seminarian who introduced him to the <a href="/wiki/Abyssinian_Baptist_Church" title="Abyssinian Baptist Church">Abyssinian Baptist Church</a> in <a href="/wiki/Harlem" title="Harlem">Harlem</a>, where Bonhoeffer taught Sunday school and formed a lifelong love for the African-American church.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He heard <a href="/wiki/Adam_Clayton_Powell_Sr." title="Adam Clayton Powell Sr.">Adam Clayton Powell Sr.</a> preach the Gospel of Social Justice, and became sensitive to the social injustices experienced by ethnic minorities in the US as well as the ineptitude of churches to bring about integration.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Through oppressed negro churches, he recognized that God's commandments were carried out and was always captivated by the sermons.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The nationalistic Bonhoeffer<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> later changed his view after seeing a film about the horrors of war.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Later, he favored the views of a pacifist and became involved with the ecumenical movement, which led him to resist Hitler and the Nazis.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Career">Career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R0211-316,_Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_mit_Sch%C3%BClern.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R0211-316%2C_Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_mit_Sch%C3%BClern.jpg/220px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R0211-316%2C_Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_mit_Sch%C3%BClern.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="160" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R0211-316%2C_Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_mit_Sch%C3%BClern.jpg/330px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R0211-316%2C_Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_mit_Sch%C3%BClern.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R0211-316%2C_Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_mit_Sch%C3%BClern.jpg/440px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R0211-316%2C_Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_mit_Sch%C3%BClern.jpg 2x" data-file-width="795" data-file-height="578" /></a><figcaption>Dietrich Bonhoeffer on a weekend getaway with <a href="/wiki/Confirmand#Protestant_views" class="mw-redirect" title="Confirmand">confirmands</a> of Zion's Church congregation (1932)<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>After returning to Germany in 1931, Bonhoeffer became a lecturer in <a href="/wiki/Systematic_theology" title="Systematic theology">systematic theology</a> at the University of Berlin. Deeply interested in <a href="/wiki/Ecumenism" title="Ecumenism">ecumenism</a>, he was appointed by the World Alliance for Promoting International Friendship through the Churches (a forerunner of the <a href="/wiki/World_Council_of_Churches" title="World Council of Churches">World Council of Churches</a>) as one of its three European youth secretaries. At this time he seems to have undergone something of a personal conversion from being a theologian primarily attracted to the intellectual side of Christianity to being a dedicated man of faith, resolved to carry out the teaching of Christ as he found it revealed in the Gospels.<sup id="cite_ref-Michael_Balfour_p._216_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Michael_Balfour_p._216-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> On 15 November 1931, at the age of 25, he was ordained at <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Church_of_the_old-Prussian_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical Church of the old-Prussian Union">Old-Prussian United</a> <a href="/w/index.php?title=St._Matthew,_Berlin-Tiergarten&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="St. Matthew, Berlin-Tiergarten (page does not exist)">St. Matthew</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/St.-Matth%C3%A4us-Kirche_(Berlin-Tiergarten)" class="extiw" title="de:St.-Matthäus-Kirche (Berlin-Tiergarten)">de</a>&#93;</span> in <a href="/wiki/Berlin-Tiergarten" class="mw-redirect" title="Berlin-Tiergarten">Berlin-Tiergarten</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Confessing_church">Confessing church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Confessing church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bonhoeffer's promising academic and ecclesiastical career was dramatically knocked off course by the Nazi ascent to power on 30 January 1933. He was a determined opponent of the regime from its first days. Two days after Hitler was installed as Chancellor, Bonhoeffer delivered a radio address in which he attacked Hitler and warned Germany against slipping into an idolatrous cult of the <i><a href="/wiki/F%C3%BChrer" title="Führer">Führer</a></i> (leader), who could very well turn out to be <i>Verführer</i> (misleader, or seducer). His broadcast was abruptly cut off, though it is unclear whether the newly elected Nazi regime was responsible.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> In April 1933, Bonhoeffer raised the first voice for church resistance to Hitler's persecution of Jews, declaring that the church must not simply "bandage the victims under the wheel, but jam a spoke in the wheel itself."<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1932, two months before the Nazi takeover, there had been an election for <a href="/wiki/Presbyter" title="Presbyter">presbyters</a> and <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synodals</a> (church officials) of the German <i>Landeskirche</i> (Protestant historical established churches). This election was marked by a struggle within the Old-Prussian Union Evangelical Church between the nationalistic <a href="/wiki/German_Christians_(movement)" title="German Christians (movement)">German Christian (Deutsche Christen) movement</a> and Young Reformers—a struggle that threatened to explode into <a href="/wiki/Schism_(religion)" class="mw-redirect" title="Schism (religion)">schism</a>. In July 1933, Hitler unconstitutionally imposed new church elections. Bonhoeffer put all his efforts into the election, campaigning for the selection of independent, non-Nazi officials.
</p><p>Despite Bonhoeffer's efforts, in the July election an overwhelming number of key church positions went to Nazi-supported <a href="/wiki/German_Christians_(movement)" title="German Christians (movement)">Deutsche Christen</a> people.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The Deutsche Christen won a majority in the general synod of the Old-Prussian Union Evangelical Church and all its provincial synods except <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Church_of_Westphalia" title="Evangelical Church of Westphalia">Westphalia</a>, and in synods of all other Protestant church bodies, except for the Lutheran churches of <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Lutheran_Church_in_Bavaria" title="Evangelical Lutheran Church in Bavaria">Bavaria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Lutheran_State_Church_of_Hanover" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical Lutheran State Church of Hanover">Hanover</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Evangelical-Lutheran_Church_in_W%C3%BCrttemberg" title="Evangelical-Lutheran Church in Württemberg">Württemberg</a>. The non-Nazi opposition regarded these bodies as uncorrupted "intact churches," as opposed to the other so-called "destroyed churches."
</p><p>In opposition to <a href="/wiki/Nazification" class="mw-redirect" title="Nazification">Nazification</a>, Bonhoeffer urged an interdict upon all pastoral services (baptisms, weddings, funerals, etc.), but <a href="/wiki/Karl_Barth" title="Karl Barth">Karl Barth</a> and others advised against such a radical proposal.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> In August 1933, Bonhoeffer and <a href="/wiki/Hermann_Sasse" title="Hermann Sasse">Hermann Sasse</a> were deputized by opposition church leaders to draft the Bethel Confession,<sup id="cite_ref-Obendiek_1992_57_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Obendiek_1992_57-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> a new statement of faith in opposition to the Deutsche Christen movement. Notable for affirming God's fidelity to Jews as His chosen people, the Bethel Confession was so watered down to make it more palatable that Bonhoeffer ultimately refused to sign it.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1933, the national church synod at <a href="/wiki/Wittenberg" title="Wittenberg">Wittenberg</a> voluntarily passed a resolution to apply the <a href="/wiki/Aryan_paragraph" title="Aryan paragraph">Aryan paragraph</a> within the church, meaning that pastors and church officials of Jewish descent were to be removed from their posts. Regarding this as an affront to the principle of <a href="/wiki/Baptism" title="Baptism">baptism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Martin_Niem%C3%B6ller" title="Martin Niemöller">Martin Niemöller</a> founded the <i><a href="/wiki/Pfarrernotbund" title="Pfarrernotbund">Pfarrernotbund</a></i> (Pastors' Emergency League). In November, a rally of 20,000 Deutsche Christens demanded the removal of the <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a> from the Bible, which was seen by many as <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heresy</a>, further swelling the ranks of the Emergency League.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Within weeks of its founding, more than a third of German pastors had joined the Emergency League. It was the forerunner of the <i><a href="/wiki/Bekennende_Kirche" class="mw-redirect" title="Bekennende Kirche">Bekennende Kirche</a></i> (Confessing Church), which aimed to preserve traditional, Biblically based Christian beliefs and practices.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Barmen_Declaration" title="Barmen Declaration">Barmen Declaration</a>, drafted by Barth in May 1934 and adopted by the Confessing Church, insisted that Christ, not the Führer, was the head of the church.<sup id="cite_ref-ushmm_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ushmm-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The adoption of the declaration has often been viewed as a triumph, although by estimate, only 20% of German pastors supported the Confessing Church.<sup id="cite_ref-LittellLocke1990_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LittellLocke1990-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ministries_in_London">Ministries in London</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Ministries in London">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>When Bonhoeffer was offered a parish post in eastern Berlin in the autumn of 1933, he refused it in protest at the nationalist policy, and accepted a two-year appointment as a pastor of two German-speaking Protestant churches in London: the <a href="/wiki/United_Evangelical_Lutheran_Church_of_Germany" title="United Evangelical Lutheran Church of Germany">German Lutheran Church</a> in Dacres Road, Sydenham,<sup id="cite_ref-ushmm_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ushmm-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> and the German Reformed Church of St Paul's, Goulston Street, Whitechapel.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> He explained to Barth that he had found little support for his views – even among friends – and that "it was about time to go for a while into the desert." Barth regarded this as running away from real battle. He sharply rebuked Bonhoeffer, saying, "I can only reply to all the reasons and excuses which you put forward: 'And what of the German Church?'" Barth accused Bonhoeffer of abandoning his post and wasting his "splendid theological armory" while "the house of your church is on fire," and chided him to return to Berlin "by the next ship".<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bonhoeffer, however, did not go to England simply to avoid trouble at home; he hoped to put the ecumenical movement to work in the interest of the Confessing Church. He continued his involvement with the Confessing Church, running up a high telephone bill to maintain his contact with Martin Niemöller. In international gatherings, Bonhoeffer rallied people to oppose the Deutsche Christen movement and its attempt to amalgamate Nazi nationalism with the Christian gospel. When Bishop <a href="/w/index.php?title=Theodor_Heckel&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Theodor Heckel (page does not exist)">Theodor Heckel</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodor_Heckel" class="extiw" title="de:Theodor Heckel">de</a>&#93;</span>—the official in charge of German Lutheran Church foreign affairs—traveled to London to warn Bonhoeffer to abstain from any ecumenical activity not directly authorized by Berlin, Bonhoeffer refused to abstain.<sup id="cite_ref-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_p._19_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_p._19-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Underground_seminaries">Underground seminaries</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Underground seminaries">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
In 1935, Bonhoeffer was offered a coveted opportunity to study non-violent resistance under <a href="/wiki/Gandhi" class="mw-redirect" title="Gandhi">Gandhi</a> in his <a href="/wiki/Ashram" title="Ashram">ashram</a>. However, remembering Barth's rebuke, Bonhoeffer decided to return to Germany instead, where he was the head at an underground seminary in <a href="/wiki/Szczecin-Zdroje" class="mw-redirect" title="Szczecin-Zdroje">Finkenwalde</a> for training Confessing Church pastors. As the Nazi suppression of the Confessing Church intensified, Barth was driven back to Switzerland in 1935; Niemöller was arrested in July 1937; and in August 1936, Bonhoeffer's authorization to teach at the <a href="/wiki/Humboldt_University_of_Berlin" title="Humboldt University of Berlin">University of Berlin</a> was revoked after he was denounced as a "<a href="/wiki/Pacifist" class="mw-redirect" title="Pacifist">pacifist</a> and enemy of the state" by Theodor Heckel.</p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dietrich_Bonhoeffer,_Skulptur_an_der_Hauptkirche_Sankt_Petri_(Hamburg).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Dietrich_Bonhoeffer%2C_Skulptur_an_der_Hauptkirche_Sankt_Petri_%28Hamburg%29.JPG/170px-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer%2C_Skulptur_an_der_Hauptkirche_Sankt_Petri_%28Hamburg%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Dietrich_Bonhoeffer%2C_Skulptur_an_der_Hauptkirche_Sankt_Petri_%28Hamburg%29.JPG/255px-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer%2C_Skulptur_an_der_Hauptkirche_Sankt_Petri_%28Hamburg%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Dietrich_Bonhoeffer%2C_Skulptur_an_der_Hauptkirche_Sankt_Petri_%28Hamburg%29.JPG/340px-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer%2C_Skulptur_an_der_Hauptkirche_Sankt_Petri_%28Hamburg%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3040" data-file-height="4560" /></a><figcaption>Memorial of Dietrich Bonhoeffer in front of <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Church,_Hamburg" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Peter&#39;s Church, Hamburg">St. Peter's Church</a>, Hamburg</figcaption></figure><p>Bonhoeffer's efforts for the underground seminaries included securing necessary funds. He found a great benefactor in <a href="/wiki/Ruth_von_Kleist-Retzow" title="Ruth von Kleist-Retzow">Ruth von Kleist-Retzow</a>. In times of trouble, Bonhoeffer's former students and their wives would take refuge in von Kleist-Retzow's Pomeranian estate, and Bonhoeffer was a frequent guest. Later he fell in love with Kleist-Retzow's granddaughter, <a href="/wiki/Maria_von_Wedemeyer" class="mw-redirect" title="Maria von Wedemeyer">Maria von Wedemeyer</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> to whom he became engaged three months before his arrest in 1943. By August 1937, <a href="/wiki/Himmler" class="mw-redirect" title="Himmler">Himmler</a> had decreed the education and examination of Confessing Church ministry candidates illegal. In September 1937, the <a href="/wiki/Gestapo" title="Gestapo">Gestapo</a> closed the seminary at Finkenwalde, and by November arrested 27 pastors and former students. It was around this time that Bonhoeffer published his best-known book, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Cost_of_Discipleship" title="The Cost of Discipleship">The Cost of Discipleship</a>,</i> a study on the <a href="/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Mount" title="Sermon on the Mount">Sermon on the Mount</a>, in which he not only attacked "cheap grace" as a cover for ethical laxity, but also preached "costly grace".
</p><p>Bonhoeffer spent the next two years secretly traveling from one eastern German village to another to conduct "seminary on the run" supervision of his students, most of whom were working illegally in small parishes within the old-Prussian <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastical_Province_of_Pomerania" class="mw-redirect" title="Ecclesiastical Province of Pomerania">Ecclesiastical Province of Pomerania</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Von_Blumenthal" class="mw-redirect" title="Von Blumenthal">von Blumenthal</a> family hosted the seminary on its estate of <a href="/wiki/S%C5%82onowice,_Pomeranian_Voivodeship" title="Słonowice, Pomeranian Voivodeship">Groß Schlönwitz</a>. The pastors of Groß Schlönwitz and neighbouring villages supported the education by employing and housing the students (among whom was Eberhard Bethge, who later edited Bonhoeffer's <i>Letters and Papers from Prison</i>) as vicars in their congregations.<sup id="cite_ref-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_1996,_p._51_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_1996,_p._51-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1938, the Gestapo banned Bonhoeffer from Berlin. In summer 1939, the seminary was able to move to Sigurdshof, an outlying estate (<a href="/wiki/Folwark" title="Folwark">Vorwerk</a>) of the <a href="/wiki/Von_Kleist" class="mw-redirect" title="Von Kleist">von Kleist</a> family in <a href="/wiki/Tychowo,_S%C5%82awno_County" title="Tychowo, Sławno County">Wendish Tychow</a>. In March 1940, the Gestapo shut down the seminary there following the outbreak of <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_1996,_p._51_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_1996,_p._51-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Bonhoeffer's monastic communal life and teaching at Finkenwalde seminary formed the basis of his books, <i>The Cost of Discipleship</i> and <i>Life Together.</i>
</p><p>Bonhoeffer's sister, Sabine, along with her Jewish-classified husband Gerhard Leibholz and their two daughters, escaped to England by way of Switzerland in 1938.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_the_United_States">Return to the United States</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Return to the United States">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In February 1938, Bonhoeffer made an initial contact with members of the <a href="/wiki/German_resistance_to_Nazism" title="German resistance to Nazism">German Resistance</a> when his brother-in-law <a href="/wiki/Hans_von_Dohn%C3%A1nyi" class="mw-redirect" title="Hans von Dohnányi">Hans von Dohnányi</a> introduced him to a group seeking Hitler's overthrow at the <a href="/wiki/Abwehr" title="Abwehr">Abwehr</a>, the German military intelligence service.
</p><p>Bonhoeffer also learned from Dohnányi that war was imminent. He was particularly troubled by the prospect of being conscripted. As a committed pacifist opposed to the Nazi regime, he could never swear an oath to Hitler and fight in his army, though refusal to do so was potentially a capital offense. He worried also about consequences his refusing military service could have for the Confessing Church, as it was a move that would be frowned upon by most Christians and their churches at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_p._19_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dietrich_Bonhoeffer_p._19-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It was at this juncture that Bonhoeffer left for the United States in June 1939 at the invitation of <a href="/wiki/Union_Theological_Seminary_(New_York_City)" title="Union Theological Seminary (New York City)">Union Theological Seminary</a> in New York. Amid much inner turmoil, he soon regretted his decision and returned after two weeks<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> despite strong pressures from his friends to stay in the United States. He wrote to <a href="/wiki/Reinhold_Niebuhr" title="Reinhold Niebuhr">Reinhold Niebuhr</a>:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I have come to the conclusion that I made a mistake in coming to America. I must live through this difficult period in our national history with the people of Germany. I will have no right to participate in the reconstruction of Christian life in Germany after the war if I do not share the trials of this time with my people&#160;... Christians in Germany will have to face the terrible alternative of either willing the defeat of their nation in order that Christian civilization may survive or willing the victory of their nation and thereby destroying civilization. I know which of these alternatives I must choose but I cannot make that choice from security.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Abwehr_agent">Abwehr agent</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Abwehr agent">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bonhoeffer-Haus_Studienzimmer.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Bonhoeffer-Haus_Studienzimmer.jpg/220px-Bonhoeffer-Haus_Studienzimmer.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Bonhoeffer-Haus_Studienzimmer.jpg/330px-Bonhoeffer-Haus_Studienzimmer.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Bonhoeffer-Haus_Studienzimmer.jpg/440px-Bonhoeffer-Haus_Studienzimmer.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1632" data-file-height="1224" /></a><figcaption>Bonhoeffer's study</figcaption></figure>
<p>Back in Germany, Bonhoeffer was further harassed by the Nazi authorities as he was forbidden to speak in public and was required regularly to report his activities to the police. In 1941, he was forbidden to print or to publish. In the meantime, Bonhoeffer had joined the Abwehr, a German military intelligence organization. Dohnányi, already part of the Abwehr, brought him into the organization on the claim that his wide ecumenical contacts would be of use to Germany, thus protecting him from conscription to active service.<sup id="cite_ref-nyrb_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyrb-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Bonhoeffer presumably knew about <a href="/wiki/Operation_Spark_(1941)" title="Operation Spark (1941)">various 1943 plots</a> against Hitler through Dohnányi, who was actively involved in the planning.<sup id="cite_ref-nyrb_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyrb-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In the face of Nazi atrocities, the full scale of which Bonhoeffer learned through the Abwehr, he concluded that "the ultimate question for a responsible man to ask is not how he is to extricate himself heroically from the affair, but how the coming generation shall continue to live."<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> He did not justify his action but accepted that he was taking guilt upon himself as he wrote, "When a man takes guilt upon himself in responsibility, he imputes his guilt to himself and no one else. He answers for it... Before other men he is justified by dire necessity; before himself he is acquitted by his conscience, but before God he hopes only for grace."<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> (In a 1932 sermon, Bonhoeffer said, "The blood of martyrs might once again be demanded, but this blood, if we really have the courage and loyalty to shed it, will not be innocent, shining like that of the first witnesses for the faith. On our blood lies heavy guilt, the guilt of the unprofitable servant who is cast into outer darkness."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>)
</p><p>Under cover of the Abwehr, Bonhoeffer served as a courier for the German resistance movement to reveal its existence and intentions to the Western Allies in hope of garnering their support, and, through his ecumenical contacts abroad, to secure possible peace terms with the Allies for a post-Hitler government. In May 1942, he met Anglican <a href="/wiki/Bishop_George_Bell" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop George Bell">Bishop George Bell</a> of Chichester, a member of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a> and an ally of the Confessing Church, contacted by Bonhoeffer's exiled brother-in-law Leibholz; through him feelers were sent to British Foreign Secretary <a href="/wiki/Sir_Anthony_Eden" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Anthony Eden">Anthony Eden</a>. However, the British government ignored these, as it had all other approaches from the German resistance.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Dohnányi and Bonhoeffer were also involved in Abwehr operations to help German Jews escape to Switzerland. During this time Bonhoeffer worked on <i><a href="/wiki/Ethics_(Bonhoeffer)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethics (Bonhoeffer)">Ethics</a></i> and wrote letters to keep up the spirits of his former students. He intended <i>Ethics</i> as his <i><a href="/wiki/Masterpiece" title="Masterpiece">magnum opus</a></i>, but it remained unfinished when he was arrested. On 5 April 1943, Bonhoeffer and Dohnányi were arrested and imprisoned.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Imprisonment">Imprisonment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Imprisonment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 13 January 1943, Bonhoeffer had become engaged to Maria von Wedemeyer, the granddaughter of his close friend and Finkenwalde seminary supporter, Ruth von Kleist Retzow. Ruth had campaigned for this marriage for several years, although up until late October 1942, Bonhoeffer remained a reluctant suitor despite Ruth being part of his innermost circle.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> A large age gap loomed between Bonhoeffer and Maria: he was 36 to her 18. Bonhoeffer had first met his would-be fiancée Maria when she was his confirmation student at age eleven.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> The two also spent almost no time alone together prior to the engagement and did not see each other between becoming engaged and Bonhoeffer's 5 April arrest. Once he was in prison, however, Maria's status as a fiancée became invaluable, as it meant she could visit Bonhoeffer and correspond with him. While their relationship was troubled,<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> she was a source of food and smuggled messages.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Bonhoeffer made <a href="/wiki/Eberhard_Bethge" title="Eberhard Bethge">Eberhard Bethge</a> his heir, but Maria, in allowing her correspondence with Bonhoeffer to be published after her death, provided an invaluable addition to the scholarship.
</p><p>For a year and a half, Bonhoeffer was imprisoned at <a href="/wiki/Tegel_Prison" title="Tegel Prison">Tegel Prison</a> awaiting trial. There he continued his work in religious outreach among his fellow prisoners and guards. Sympathetic guards helped smuggle his letters out of prison to <a href="/wiki/Eberhard_Bethge" title="Eberhard Bethge">Eberhard Bethge</a> and others, and these uncensored letters were posthumously published in <i>Letters and Papers from Prison</i>. One of those guards, a corporal named Knobloch, even offered to help him escape from the prison and "disappear" with him, and plans were made for that end but Bonhoeffer declined it, fearing Nazi retribution against his family, especially his brother Klaus and brother-in-law Hans von Dohnányi, who was also imprisoned.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 4 April 1945, the diaries of Admiral <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_Canaris" title="Wilhelm Canaris">Wilhelm Canaris</a>, head of the Abwehr, were discovered, and in a rage upon reading them, Hitler ordered that the Abwehr conspirators be destroyed.<sup id="cite_ref-PlottingHitler_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PlottingHitler-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Bonhoeffer was led away just as he concluded his final Sunday service and asked an English prisoner, <a href="/wiki/Sigismund_Payne_Best" title="Sigismund Payne Best">Payne Best</a>, to remember him to Bishop George Bell of Chichester if he should ever reach his home: "This is the end—for me the beginning of life."<sup id="cite_ref-Eberhard_Bethge_p._927_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eberhard_Bethge_p._927-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Execution">Execution</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Execution">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Flossenb%C3%BCrg_April_9_1945_Memorial.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Flossenb%C3%BCrg_April_9_1945_Memorial.JPG/170px-Flossenb%C3%BCrg_April_9_1945_Memorial.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="261" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Flossenb%C3%BCrg_April_9_1945_Memorial.JPG/255px-Flossenb%C3%BCrg_April_9_1945_Memorial.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Flossenb%C3%BCrg_April_9_1945_Memorial.JPG/340px-Flossenb%C3%BCrg_April_9_1945_Memorial.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1458" data-file-height="2241" /></a><figcaption>Flossenbürg concentration camp, Arrestblock-Hof: Memorial to members of German resistance executed on 9 April 1945</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bonhoeffer was sentenced to death on 8 April 1945 by SS judge <a href="/wiki/Otto_Thorbeck" title="Otto Thorbeck">Otto Thorbeck</a> at a <a href="/wiki/Drumhead_court-martial" title="Drumhead court-martial">drumhead court-martial</a> without witnesses, records of proceedings or a defense in <a href="/wiki/Flossenb%C3%BCrg_concentration_camp" title="Flossenbürg concentration camp">Flossenbürg concentration camp</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-GermanResistance_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GermanResistance-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> He was executed there by hanging at dawn on 9 April 1945. Bonhoeffer was stripped of his clothing and led naked into the execution yard where he was hanged with six others: Admiral <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_Canaris" title="Wilhelm Canaris">Wilhelm Canaris</a>; General <a href="/wiki/Hans_Oster" title="Hans Oster">Hans Oster</a>, Canaris's deputy; General <a href="/wiki/Karl_Sack" title="Karl Sack">Karl Sack</a>, a military jurist; businessman <a href="/wiki/Theodor_Str%C3%BCnck" title="Theodor Strünck">Theodor Strünck</a>; and German resistance fighter <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_Gehre" title="Ludwig Gehre">Ludwig Gehre</a>.
</p>
<p><a href="/wiki/Eberhard_Bethge" title="Eberhard Bethge">Eberhard Bethge</a>, a student and friend of Bonhoeffer, writes of a man who saw the execution: </p><blockquote><p>I saw Pastor Bonhoeffer... kneeling on the floor praying fervently to God. I was most deeply moved by the way this lovable man prayed, so devout and so certain that God heard his prayer. At the place of execution, he again said a short prayer and then climbed the few steps to the gallows, brave and composed. His death ensued after a few seconds. In the almost fifty years that I worked as a doctor, I have hardly ever seen a man die so entirely submissive to the will of God.<sup id="cite_ref-Eberhard_Bethge_p._927_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eberhard_Bethge_p._927-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>This is the traditional account of Bonhoeffer's death, which over the decades went unchallenged;<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> however, many recent biographers see problems with the story, not due to Bethge but his source. The purported witness was a doctor at Flossenbürg concentration camp, Hermann Fischer-Hüllstrung,<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> who may have wished to minimize the suffering of the condemned men to reduce his own culpability in their executions. J.L.F. Mogensen, a former prisoner at Flossenbürg, cited the length of time it took for the execution to be completed (almost six hours), plus departures from camp procedure that would probably not have been allowed to prisoners so late in the war, as jarring inconsistencies. Considering that the sentences had been confirmed at the highest levels of Nazi government, by individuals with a pattern of torturing prisoners who dared to challenge the regime, it is more likely that "the physical details of Bonhoeffer's death may have been much more difficult than we earlier had imagined."<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other recent critics of the traditional account are more caustic. One terms the Fischer-Hüllstrung story as "unfortunately a lie," citing additional factual inconsistencies; for example, the doctor described Bonhoeffer climbing the steps to the noose, but at Flossenbürg the gallows had no steps.  Moreover, it appears that "Fischer-Hüllstrung had the job of reviving political prisoners after they had been hanged until they were almost dead, in order to prolong the agony of their dying."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchlingensiepen2010406_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchlingensiepen2010406-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Another critic charges that Fischer-Hüllstrung's "subsequent statement about Bonhoeffer as kneeling in wordy prayer ... belongs to the realm of legend."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKaiser2014311_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaiser2014311-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The disposition of Bonhoeffer's remains is not known.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarsh2014390_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarsh2014390-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> His body may have been cremated outside the camp along with hundreds of other recently executed or dead prisoners,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchlingensiepen2010378_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchlingensiepen2010378-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> or American troops may have placed his body in one of several mass graves in which they interred the unburied dead of the camp.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarsh2014390_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarsh2014390-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Westminster_Abbey_C20th_martyrs.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Westminster_Abbey_C20th_martyrs.jpg/220px-Westminster_Abbey_C20th_martyrs.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Westminster_Abbey_C20th_martyrs.jpg/330px-Westminster_Abbey_C20th_martyrs.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Westminster_Abbey_C20th_martyrs.jpg/440px-Westminster_Abbey_C20th_martyrs.jpg 2x" data-file-width="525" data-file-height="398" /></a><figcaption>Gallery of 20th Century Martyrs at <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster Abbey</a>. From left, <a href="/wiki/Princess_Elisabeth_of_Hesse_and_by_Rhine_(1864%E2%80%931918)" title="Princess Elisabeth of Hesse and by Rhine (1864–1918)">Mother Elizabeth of Russia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr." title="Martin Luther King Jr.">Martin Luther King Jr.</a>, <a href="/wiki/%C3%93scar_Romero" title="Óscar Romero">Óscar Romero</a> and Dietrich Bonhoeffer.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bonhoeffer is commemorated in the <a href="/wiki/Liturgical_calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Liturgical calendar">liturgical calendars</a> of several Christian denominations on the anniversary of his death, 9 April. This includes many parts of the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>, where he is sometimes identified as a martyr,<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> and other times not.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
His commemoration in the liturgical calendar of the <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Lutheran_Church_in_America" title="Evangelical Lutheran Church in America">Evangelical Lutheran Church in America</a> uses the <a href="/wiki/Liturgical_color" class="mw-redirect" title="Liturgical color">liturgical color</a> of white,<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> which is typically used for non-martyred saints.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
In 2008, the General Conference of the <a href="/wiki/United_Methodist_Church" title="United Methodist Church">United Methodist Church</a>, which does not enumerate saints, officially <a href="/wiki/Saints_in_Methodism" title="Saints in Methodism">recognized</a> Bonhoeffer as a "modern-day <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a>". He was the first martyr to be so recognized who lived after the <a href="/wiki/Reformation" title="Reformation">Reformation</a>, and is one of only two as of 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bonhoeffer is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Commemoration_(observance)" class="mw-redirect" title="Commemoration (observance)">commemoration</a> on <a href="/wiki/April_9" title="April 9">9 April</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Deutsche Evangelische Kirche in Sydenham, London, at which he preached between 1933 and 1935, was destroyed by bombing in 1944. A replacement church was built in 1958 and named <i>Dietrich-Bonhoeffer-Kirche</i> in his honor.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Dietrich Bonhoeffer's text '<a href="/wiki/Von_guten_M%C3%A4chten" title="Von guten Mächten">Von guten Mächten</a>' (translated as 'By Gentle' or 'By Gracious Powers') is known to a large audience as a worship song. The song is often sung at funerals. In 2021 it was voted the most popular hymn in Germany. The best-known melody was written by <a href="/wiki/Siegfried_Fietz" title="Siegfried Fietz">Siegfried Fietz</a> in 1970.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Theological_legacy">Theological legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Dietrich_Bonhoeffer&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Theological legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output
